are constantly evolving, and so are the techniques they use to bypass \u003ca href=\"https://security.googleblog.com/2024/07/improving-security-of-chrome-cookies-on.html\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eApplication-Bound Encryption (ABE)\u003c/a\u003e. In recent weeks, Vidar has been among the most actively developed stealers and, apart from \u003ca href=\"https://x.com/GenThreatLabs/status/2064836539952361976\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003emultiple updates to its string obfuscation\u003c/a\u003e and \u003ca href=\"https://x.com/GenThreatLabs/status/2061462524604649840\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003ea reworked approach to protecting its configuration\u003c/a\u003e, it has also introduced a novel technique for bypassing ABE. And while there have been many other changes in Vidar lately, with new versions dropping every week, in this blog post we focus solely on the ABE bypass and its technical aspects.\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe ABE Bypass\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom a high-level perspective, Vidar’s approach to bypassing ABE is somewhat similar to that of Remus/Lumma, in that both try to extract the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e directly from the browser’s memory. What sets them apart, however, is how they achieve it. Since we’ve already covered the inner workings of ABE in earlier posts (\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/remus-64bit-variant-of-lumma-stealer\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eRemus\u003c/a\u003e, \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/voidstealer-abe-bypass\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eVoidStealer\u003c/a\u003e), we don’t revisit them here and focus instead on the bypass itself. For readers interested in the broader background, we encourage checking those earlier posts.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn short, the end goal of infostealers is to obtain the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e, as it alone is sufficient for decrypting any ABE-protected data tied to a specific application. This key can be obtained in one of two ways: either from disk, where it is protected by two layers of \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectData\u003c/code\u003e (one applied in the context of the logged-in user and one as \u003ccode\u003eNT AUTHORITY\\SYSTEM\u003c/code\u003e), or from the browser’s memory – the direction Vidar took.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn memory, however, the key is kept protected with \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e using the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag. This ensures that even if an attacker manages to read the encrypted form of the key from memory, they cannot simply decrypt it (they would need to invoke the complementary \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e function directly from within the browser process). That leaves attackers with two problems to solve: how to find the encrypted key in memory, and how to get it decrypted.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eLocating the v20_master_key in Browser’s Memory\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eVidar’s approach to locating the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e in memory involves scanning the entire memory of a running browser for a specific pattern. But that first requires a browser process to scan.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the target browser is already running, Vidar reuses the existing process. However, it doesn’t read its memory directly. Instead, it creates a fork of it using \u003ccode\u003eNtCreateProcessEx\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003eSectionHandle\u003c/code\u003e set to \u003ccode\u003eNULL\u003c/code\u003e, instructing the kernel to clone the address space of the process specified by the \u003ccode\u003eParentProcess\u003c/code\u003e as a copy-on-write mapping. The resulting process has no threads and is never resumed – it exists purely as a static memory snapshot.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo perform the fork, Vidar first opens a handle to the target browser via \u003ccode\u003eOpenProcess\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_QUERY_INFORMATION | PROCESS_CREATE_PROCESS | PROCESS_VM_READ\u003c/code\u003e, falling back to \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_CREATE_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e if the initial call fails. It then passes this handle as the \u003ccode\u003eParentProcess\u003c/code\u003e argument to \u003ccode\u003eNtCreateProcessEx\u003c/code\u003e, requesting \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_ALL_ACCESS\u003c/code\u003e on the resulting child. To read it, Vidar creates a second fork of the browser process and reads the key from the same address using \u003ccode\u003eNtReadVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e. It then compares the result against a snapshot of the encrypted key taken from the first fork before the APC call – if the data is unchanged, decryption failed, and Vidar moves on to the next browser process’s candidate.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf the data differs, Vidar uses the same second fork to perform an additional verification: it scans the forked process’s memory for the byte sequence \u003ccode\u003ev20\u003c/code\u003e and, treating each match as a potentially ABE-encrypted entry (cookie, password), attempts AES-256-GCM decryption with the key candidate using \u003ccode\u003eBCryptDecrypt\u003c/code\u003e (successful GCM tag verification confirms that the key is correct).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhenever the data has changed (indicating that decryption of the key took place), Vidar re-encrypts the key in the browser’s memory by injecting another APC with \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e as the routine, preserving the browser’s state. Is the refund message real? ","created":"1781722434","subtitle":"Travel disruption gives scammers the one thing they need most: a believable reason to rush you","authors":[{"id":"15036","socialLinks":[],"bio":"At Gen, Luis tracks evolving threats and trends, turning research into actionable safety advice. He has worked in cybersecurity since 1999. He chairs the AMTSO Board and serves on the Board of MUTE.","displayName":"Luis Corrons","title":"Security Evangelist at Gen","picture":{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_large/public/blog-author-pictures/LuisCorronsHeadshot.jpg.webp?h=0c1d77dd\u0026itok=ingDMIDp 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_large_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/LuisCorronsHeadshot.jpg.webp?h=0c1d77dd\u0026itok=hOkaUSXZ 2x","media":"(min-width: 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_small_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/LuisCorronsHeadshot.jpg.webp?h=0c1d77dd\u0026itok=vXd2nscc 2x","media":"(min-width: 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":120,"height":120}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small/public/blog-author-pictures/LuisCorronsHeadshot.jpg.webp?h=0c1d77dd\u0026itok=I19sJFlI"}}],"contributingAuthors":[],"products":[],"paragraphs":[{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eYou have a summer flight booked. You have seen the headlines about jet fuel shortages, cancelled routes and higher travel costs. Then a text message arrives: your flight has been affected, and you need to click a link to confirm your refund or rebook before the offer expires.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat message feels plausible because the situation around it is real. In April, \u003ca href=\"https://www.iata.org/en/pressroom/2026-releases/2026-04-17-01/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eIATA warned\u003c/a\u003e that Europe could start seeing flight cancellations by the end of May if jet fuel shortages materialized. Airlines and governments have since been trying to reassure travelers, while also publishing guidance on passenger rights, refunds and re-routing.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is exactly the kind of confusion scammers like. They do not need to cause the disruption, they only need to arrive at the right moment, with the right brand logo and the right sense of urgency.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhy travel disruption works so well as a scam hook\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost scam messages fail because they arrive out of context. A random refund email from an airline you never use is easy to ignore. A refund message from the airline you are flying with next week, during a period of public travel disruption, is different.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe scammer’s job is to make you think: “This could be real.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat can happen in several ways. You might receive a fake cancellation email that links to a refund form. You might search for an airline’s customer service number and land on a fake support page. You might post publicly on social media asking an airline for help, then receive a reply from an account that looks official. Or you might get a WhatsApp message that knows your hotel name, travel dates or booking reference.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach version uses the same pressure point: you are worried about losing money, losing your flight or being stranded.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCriminals are already using disruption this way. In March, \u003ca href=\"https://www.theguardian.com/money/2026/mar/15/travel-scam-airline-accounts-fake-refunds-iran-war-flight-disruption\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eThe Guardian reported\u003c/a\u003e that fake airline support accounts were targeting travelers affected by Middle East flight disruption. Some victims were looking for help or refunds, then received replies from accounts pretending to represent airlines. The scam moved into direct messages, where victims were asked for details and sent links that led to money being taken instead of refunded.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe jet fuel story gives scammers another pretext. A message can claim that your flight was cancelled, your fare changed, your fuel surcharge failed, or your refund must be claimed within a few hours. None of those claims needs to be true to be effective.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eThe scam may start before the message arrives\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eA fake travel message becomes much harder to spot when it includes real information.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the idea behind \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/reservation-hijack-scam\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eReservation Hijack scams\u003c/a\u003e, a form of targeted phishing where cybercriminals use real booking details to make a message look legitimate. In some cases, attackers may use information exposed through breaches or compromised travel systems. In other cases, they collect details from emails, social media posts, previous leaks or fake customer service interactions. Gen has previously described Reservation Hijack scams as scams that use real hotel booking details to make fraudulent payment or verification requests feel authentic.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat distinction matters. A scam message does not need your credit card number to fool you. Knowing your name, your destination, your travel dates and your booking platform may be enough.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat these scams can look like\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eA travel disruption scam usually does not begin with a strange offer. It begins with something boring and administrative. That is part of the trick.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eYou may see messages like:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Your flight has been cancelled due to fuel supply issues. Claim your refund here.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Your rebooking is pending. Confirm passenger details within two hours.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Additional airport tax or fuel surcharge is required to keep your booking.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Your hotel reservation will be cancelled unless payment is verified.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Due to high call volume, please continue this request on WhatsApp.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe exact wording changes, but the structure is familiar. There is a problem, a deadline and a link. The message may also move you away from the official channel into SMS, WhatsApp, Telegram, direct messages or a phone call. Once that happens, the scammer controls the conversation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is another detail worth watching: some messages do not ask for money immediately. They start by asking for your booking reference, last name, phone number or one-time password. That can still be dangerous. Those details can help scammers access a booking, impersonate you to customer support, reset an account or make the next message more convincing.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eYour real passenger rights can be used against you\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf an airline cancels your flight, you may have rights to a refund, re-routing or assistance, depending on where you are travelling and the rules that apply. The European Commission said passengers affected by cancellations continue to have rights to reimbursement, re-routing or return, airport assistance and compensation for last-minute cancellations in relevant circumstances. It also said airlines cannot retroactively add fuel surcharges to already published final ticket prices.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat information is useful for travelers, but it is also useful for scammers.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA fake refund message becomes more believable when people already know refunds are possible. A fake rebooking link becomes more believable when airlines are genuinely changing schedules. A fake “fuel surcharge” demand becomes more dangerous when fuel prices are in the news, even though legitimate retroactive charges may not be allowed in many cases.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is why the safest route is not to judge the message only by whether the story sounds plausible. During travel disruption, many scam stories will sound plausible. Judge the channel instead.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eHow to check before you click\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eStart from the airline or booking platform’s official app or website. Do not use the link in the message. Open the app yourself, type the website address yourself, or use the contact details listed on your original booking confirmation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCheck whether the same cancellation, refund or payment request appears inside your account. If the message is real, the airline or hotel should be able to confirm it through an official channel.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBe careful with social media support. If an account replies to your public complaint, check whether it is linked from the airline’s official website. Look at the handle carefully. Fake support accounts often copy logos and names, but have small differences in spelling, low follower counts or recently created profiles. The Guardian reported that fraudsters often scan social media for passengers trying to contact legitimate airline accounts and reply to them first, which makes the scam harder to spot.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDo not share one-time passwords, payment card details or login credentials with anyone who contacts you unexpectedly. A real airline or hotel does not need your banking OTP to send you a refund.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBe especially suspicious of pressure. “You have 30 minutes to claim your refund” is not customer service. It is a tactic.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat to do if you already clicked\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you entered payment details, contact your bank or card provider immediately. Ask whether the transaction can be blocked or reversed, and request a new card if needed.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you shared login details for an airline, hotel or booking platform, change the password from the official site or app. Use a unique password, not one you use elsewhere. Turn on two-factor authentication where it is available.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you shared a one-time password or authentication code, assume the account may have been accessed. Check your booking details, payment methods and recent account activity.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eReport the scam to the airline, hotel or booking platform being impersonated. If the scam came through social media, report the account as impersonation. If money was taken, report it to the relevant fraud authority in your country.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eThe real lesson for summer travel\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eTravel disruption changes how people behave. They search for support numbers in a hurry. They post publicly because customer service queues are long. They accept strange instructions because they are worried about losing a flight or refund.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eScammers build around that behavior.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe safest assumption is simple: if a message about your flight, hotel or refund creates urgency and gives you a link, pause. Go back to the official source. A real cancellation will still be there when you check through the airline or booking platform. A scammer needs you to act before you do.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"8","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-06/threat%20flight%20canceled%20image.jpg?h=8c9c35ae\u0026itok=33YI5TgM","relatedPosts":[{"urlAlias":"/blog-post/fake-invoices-shopping-apps","title":"Fake invoices are moving from inboxes to shopping apps ","subtitle":"Scammers are using order-tracking apps to place fake receipts where users expect to see real purchases, then pushing them to call fake support numbers. fake job interview page is easier to trust than a fake ticket shop.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn samples reviewed by Gen threat researchers, scammers used branded hiring pages that looked like ordinary recruitment flows: a company logo, a recruiter profile, a 30-minute meeting, and a button to continue with Google or Facebook. There was no attachment to open and no software to install. The credential theft attempt sat inside a step many job applicants already expect: signing in to schedule a call.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFIFA is an obvious lure ahead of the 2026 World Cup. On May 22, ESET reported fake FIFA-themed websites that copied ticket and merchandise flows to steal money and personal data. Our researchers later found another FIFA-themed domain, hxxps://fifa-mg[.]shop, which followed a naming pattern not mentioned in that report. Domains like these can be short-lived, often appearing for hours or days before they are blocked, abandoned, or taken down.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur researchers also found a different kind of FIFA lure: hxxps://fifahiring[.]com, a fake hiring page that encouraged users to schedule a call with an alleged FIFA recruiter. When a user tried to register with a personal Gmail address, the page asked for a business email instead. The page was steering victims toward credentials with more value: accounts that could provide access to a company flexibility makes the campaign harder to frame as a single World Cup scam. FIFA provides the timely hook, but the mechanism is recruitment phishing. A scammer can swap the logo, recruiter name, and sign-in provider while keeping the same basic path to the victim’s credentials.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhy fake recruitment pages work\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eRecruitment gives attackers a clean excuse to ask for things that might otherwise raise suspicion.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA candidate expects to click a scheduling link. They expect a recruiter profile. They expect a calendar prompt. In some cases, they may also expect to use a work email, especially for corporate, contractor, or partner-facing roles.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fake login window turns that ordinary flow into credential theft. Users are used to “Continue with Google” and “Continue with Facebook” buttons, but the button itself proves nothing. A genuine authentication flow should take the user to the provider’s domain and controls. A fake page can draw a convincing copy while keeping the victim inside the attacker-controlled site.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe business email prompt is also a warning sign. A page that rejects a personal email and asks for a work address may be trying to raise the value of the stolen account.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat job seekers should check before signing in\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eStart from the company’s official careers page, not from a link in a message, ad, search result, or forwarded post. For FIFA, that means checking jobs.fifa.com or fifa.pinpointhq.com, not trusting a domain because it contains “fifa,” “career,” “hiring,” or “jobs.”\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eLook closely at the domain before entering credentials. Scam pages often add plausible hiring words around a brand name: career, talent, jobs, hiring, portal, or connect. A polished page on a new domain is still a new domain.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTreat the sign-in step as the real checkpoint. If a page offers Google or Facebook login, check whether the flow actually opens a real provider-controlled authentication page. A fake window embedded inside the original site, broken controls, inactive links, or a provider logo used only as decoration should stop the process.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eUse security software that includes web and phishing protection. These tools can block known malicious pages before they load, which is especially useful when scam domains look polished, appear in search results, or are shared through otherwise legitimate channels.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat companies can do\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eRecruitment pages are public by design, which makes them easy to copy.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSecurity teams should monitor for newly registered domains combining their brand with hiring-related terms. They should also watch for pages that reuse favicons, recruiter photos, job descriptions, and calendar-booking language from legitimate hiring flows.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHR and recruiting teams should make the official hiring path easy to verify. Candidates should not have to guess whether a domain is legitimate. A clear careers page, consistent recruiter communication, and warnings about unofficial scheduling links can remove some of the ambiguity attackers rely on.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eConclusion\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fake FIFA hiring page worked because it did not feel unusual. It looked like a normal step in a hiring process.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAttackers do not need to invent new behavior when they can copy one people already trust. A branded scheduling page, a recruiter profile, and a familiar sign-in button can be enough to move a victim from interest to credential entry.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe decision point is the login. Before entering a work email or password, check the domain, check where the authentication flow really goes, and verify the role through the company’s official careers site. Security software with web and phishing protection adds another layer, especially against short-lived scam pages that may only stay online for hours or days.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eIOCs:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://fifahiring[.]com\u003cbr\u003ehxxps://careers-fifahiring[.]com\u003cbr\u003ehxxps://fifajobs[.]com\u003cbr\u003ehxxps://fifa-mg[.]shop\u003cbr\u003ehxxps://heineken-careers[.]com\u003cbr\u003ehxxps://aquent-talent-jobs[.]com\u003cbr\u003ehxxps://hays-talent-opportunities[.]com\u003cbr\u003efifeq2026eqbackeq[.]onrender[.]com/api/login\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"8","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-06/fifa%20hero%20image.jpg?h=7a69d888\u0026itok=fTc68Mu2","relatedPosts":[{"urlAlias":"/blog-post/fake-invoices-shopping-apps","title":"Fake invoices are moving from inboxes to shopping apps ","subtitle":"Scammers are using order-tracking apps to place fake receipts where users expect to see real purchases, then pushing them to call fake support numbers. Lead","picture":{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_large/public/blog-author-pictures/Untitled%20design_2.jpg.webp?h=6a4a5c49\u0026itok=203g40mw 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_large_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/Untitled%20design_2.jpg.webp?h=6a4a5c49\u0026itok=ObrRQ8Pr 2x","media":"(min-width: 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_small_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/Untitled%20design_2.jpg.webp?h=6a4a5c49\u0026itok=OEVdkY1W 2x","media":"(min-width: January 2026, we have detected 7.4 million malicious incidents tied to residential proxy traffic, affecting 572,000 users in our telemetry. In this model, the household whose connection is being used is often not the buyer of the traffic. It is the exit point.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is the uncomfortable part of residential proxy networks. A home router, laptop, phone, smart TV, or other always-on device can quietly become part of a commercial network that routes strangers’ internet traffic through ordinary consumer IP addresses. To the websites on the other end, that traffic looks as if it came from a real household, because it did.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe technology itself is not inherently malicious. Businesses use residential proxies for market research, ad verification, brand protection, security testing, and services that need to see the web as a real user would. But the same feature that makes these networks useful also makes them attractive for abuse: traffic from a household IP address is hard to block without risking false positives against legitimate users.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe problem for consumers is simpler. Many people do not realize their device or connection can be enrolled into this kind of network at all. Some users opt in through pay-for-bandwidth apps. Others are enrolled through “free” VPNs, bundled software development kits inside unrelated apps, or unwanted software that installs proxy components without meaningful consent.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHere is how the market works, who pays for it, and what it can cost the household that unknowingly hosts it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat is a residential proxy\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eA residential proxy is an internet connection – typically a home laptop, phone, smart TV, or router – that has been enrolled into a third-party network and is being used to route someone else's traffic. To the rest of the internet, that traffic looks like it is coming from an ordinary household IP address assigned by a consumer ISP, not from a datacenter or\u0026nbsp;VPN provider.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eResidential proxy networks are sold commercially. The model is two-sided: some people pay to tunnel their traffic out through someone else's connection (much like a VPN, except the exit point is a real home), while others get paid to provide those exit endpoints. Some are structured as paid peer-to-peer networks, where any participant's connection can serve as an exit for any other.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBuyers pay for access to pools of millions of these \"real user\" IPs because they are extremely difficult to block, or rather, blocking them carries real consequences: the traffic originates from genuine households, so any block risks shutting out a legitimate user – effectively a false positive.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCommon buyers include market research firms, price-comparison services, brand-protection vendors, security teams testing their own defenses, and consumer privacy tools. At the abusive end of the market, the same infrastructure used by operators running automated purchasing bots, scrapping campaigns, ad fraud, credential stuffing, phishing and other attacks where hiding the true origin of the traffic matters. Demand is also being shaped by large-scale web scraping, including\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://blog.includesecurity.com/2026/06/the-smart-tv-in-your-livingroom-is-a-node-in-the-aiscraping-economy/\"\u003escraping for AI training data\u003c/a\u003e. As anti-bot systems become better at identifying traffic from datacenters and known VPN providers, residential IP addresses become more attractive because they look like ordinary users. That does not make every residential proxy user malicious. It does mean consumers may be carrying traffic for businesses and actors they have never heard of.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow consumer devices end up in these networks\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere are several ways a home device can become part of a residential proxy network. Some are explicit. Others are easy to miss.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe most obvious route is the pay-for-bandwidth model. Apps such as Honeygain, IPRoyal Pawns, and PacketStream pay users to share their internet connection. The user opts in, but they may not understand who will buy access to that connection later, what kind of traffic will pass through it, or what happens if that traffic triggers abuse reports.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA second route is the “free” VPN or proxy app. Instead of charging a subscription, the app monetizes the user’s connection by reselling bandwidth. In practice, the user may think they are getting a privacy tool while also becoming infrastructure for other people’s traffic.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA third route is through bundled software development kits, or SDKs. A developer adds a third-party component to an otherwise unrelated app, such as a flashlight, wallpaper, game, or utility. The developer gets paid when the app is active. The user sees the host app, not the proxy network behind it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fourth route is malware or potentially unwanted software. In those cases, the proxy component is installed silently or deceptively, with no meaningful user consent at all.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy the \"consent\" is rarely meaningful\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eEven when consent technically exists, it is often not the kind of consent most people would recognize.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe disclosure may be buried in a long terms-of-service document. The wording may be vague, with phrases such as “share your unused bandwidth,” “support the app,” or “join our network.” These phrases do not clearly tell the user that arbitrary third parties may route traffic through the user’s home IP address.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is also the question of downstream use. Users are rarely told who buys access to the network or what those buyers do with it. Their connection could be used for benign market research. It could also be used for scraping, automated account creation, ad fraud, credential stuffing, or phishing infrastructure.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe problem gets worse when the proxy component sits inside another app. The host app’s privacy policy may mention a partner SDK by brand name, but that still does not make it clear to a normal user that their device can become a proxy endpoint.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eUninstalling the visible app may not always resolve the issue cleanly either, especially when proxy components are installed separately or bundled with other unwanted software.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe risks consumers actually carry\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen a household device becomes a residential proxy node, the user takes on risks created by someone else’s traffic.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe first risk is attribution. Abusive, fraudulent, or illegal activity routed through the device appears to come from the household’s IP address. ISP abuse notices, platform bans, fraud checks, and law enforcement requests point first to the subscriber, not to the person who bought the proxy traffic.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe second risk is performance. Continuous third-party traffic can consume bandwidth, slow the home network, hit data caps, or violate ISP terms of service. The user may only notice that the internet feels slower, a bill is higher, or a service suddenly blocks access.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe third risk is security exposure. Proxy clients run persistent background processes and maintain network connections so they can relay traffic. That adds always-on networking software to the device, often with limited visibility for the user.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fourth risk is reputation. A home IP address used for abuse can end up on blocklists used by banks, retailers, streaming services, and online platforms. The result can be account friction, failed logins, extra verification steps, or outright blocking from services the household actually uses.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is also a family angle. A child or another household member may install a free game, VPN, or utility without understanding the consequences. The legal, financial, and reputational risk still lands on the account holder.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat our telemetry shows\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eSince the beginning of 2026, we have detected 7.4 million malicious traffic incidents associated with residential proxy traffic, affecting 572,000 unique users in our user base.\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eThis is not a measurement of the entire residential proxy market. It reflects what we observe in our telemetry: malicious traffic, detections, and related activity affecting protected users. The real footprint is far larger, because residential proxy networks span major consumer platforms and many devices outside our visibility.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe activity is global, but it is concentrated in a smaller set of countries. India leads by a wide margin, with roughly 83,000 affected users, followed by Vietnam with 61,000 and Brazil with 39,000. The Philippines (23,000), Poland (18,000), Indonesia (18,000) and Thailand (14,000) form a second tier, with Egypt, Spain, Ukraine, Argentina, Mexico and Italy each accounting for 10,000 to 13,000. The spread across South and Southeast Asia, Latin America, and Eastern and Southern Europe reflects where the host apps that carry this software are most widely installed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat geographic spread likely reflects a combination of app distribution, local install patterns, user appetite for free or pay-for-bandwidth apps, and the economics of bandwidth resale. It should not be read as a simple ranking of where residential proxy abuse originates.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe chart below breaks down the threats we observed in this proxy-related traffic. Two categories dominate: phishing and malicious advertising. Behind them sits a long tail of consumer-facing fraud, including e-shop scams, generic scams, financial scams, and fake-tutorial pages, alongside outright malware such as trojans and other malicious traffic.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-06/Threat%20Breakdown%20of%20Residential%20Proxy%20Traffic%20%285%29.png.webp?itok=Qo_9Av-O the malicious proxy traffic we observed, scams and malware dominate. The important point for consumers is not only that their connection may be used by someone else. It is that, when abuse happens, their household IP address can be the return address.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat consumers can do\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost people will not identify a residential proxy component by name. But there are practical warning signs.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eReview “free” VPNs, proxy apps, bandwidth-sharing apps, and utilities from unknown publishers. Check whether they mention bandwidth sharing, peer-to-peer networking, traffic routing, or network participation in their terms. If an app offers payment for leaving your device online, assume your connection may be resold unless the provider explains exactly how traffic is vetted and who can buy access.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn phones and computers, remove apps you do not recognize or no longer use. On home routers, check the list of connected devices and look for anything unfamiliar. If your ISP sends abuse notices, websites start blocking you, or your home IP address suddenly fails fraud checks, a proxy component is one possible explanation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSecurity software can also help by detecting unwanted proxy clients, bundled components, and related malicious activity. But this should not be a guessing game for consumers. Software that turns a household connection into a commercial exit node should be named clearly and made easy to remove.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe takeaway\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith residential proxiesthe user’s connection becomes an infrastructure for someone else’s business.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe issue is not that residential proxy technology exists. It has legitimate uses. The issue is whether people know when their devices are part of it, understand what kind of traffic may pass through their connection, and have a real choice to opt out.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fix is not to pretend consumers will read every EULA. This software should be named clearly, shown plainly, and made easy to remove before a household connection becomes someone else’s infrastructure.\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"9","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-06/ChatGPT%20Image%20Jun%2011%2C%202026%2C%2010_30_11%20AM.jpg?h=10cbc464\u0026itok=C_Uxd7UF","relatedPosts":[{"urlAlias":"/blog-post/fake-invoices-shopping-apps","title":"Fake invoices are moving from inboxes to shopping apps ","subtitle":"Scammers are using order-tracking apps to place fake receipts where users expect to see real purchases, then pushing them to call fake support numbers.
